A new edition of the cuneiform text of the Epic of Gilgamish, with due arrangement of the 
fragments, has long been wanted, owing first to the discovery of much new material, and 
secondly to the fact that most of the editions of the text of the Assyrian Version are now out 
of print. Moreover, partly owing to the difficulty of settling the order of many of the 
fragments, a complete edition of a composite text of the Epic arranged for those who wished 
to read the original cuneiform as a consecutive story has never been made, and in order to 
produce such a text for students I began some few years ago, with the permission of the 
authorities of the British Museum, to collate and re-copy the known tablets of this series, at 
the same time examining several hundreds of fragments of unidentified texts in the Kouyunjik 
Collection which might possibly provide duplicates or ‘joins’. In my search I was fairly 
successful, and not only with these but also with the known fragments of the Epic I was able 
to make new ‘joins’, and these results, together with comparisons with other published texts 
of the Epic of different periods, threw new light on the proper sequence of various stray 
fragments of the Epic, whereof the order in the series had still been in doubt. It was thus 
reasonable to try to arrange the texts of the great story as a composite whole in sequence, in 
which the relative position of only a few fragments is now precarious. A transliteration with 
such brief notes as were deemed necessary has been added, my translation, Zhe Epic of 
Gilgamish (Luzac & Co.), having already been published in 1928. 

The discovery of the Epic and the first arrangement and translation of its material was 
due to the genius of George Smith, although attention had early been drawn by Sir Henry 
Rawlinson to the probability that the accounts in Genesis had a Babylonian origin, and that 
Gilgamish was a solar hero.’ During Smith’s work in preparation of the Fourth Volume of 
Cuneiform Inscriptions of Western Asia, the famous series of cuneiform texts published by the 
Trustees of the British Museum under the editorship of Rawlinson, he had collected the 
fragments from Nineveh which seemed to him to relate to mythological subjects, and, in doing 
so, recognized that the contents of one tablet were obviously the same as the Flood story of 
the Old Testament. This Assyrian story of the Flood he described in a paper to the Society 
of Biblical Archaeology on 3 December 1872, thereafter laying the base of our knowledge of 
the Epic; and by the time of the appearance of his ‘Chaldean Account of Genesis’ in 1875, he 
had recognized that the Epic contained twelve tablets, and estimated that the age of the 
legends in their present form was about 2000 B.c., an opinion which the discovery of the 
three tablets of the Old Babylonian period has amply justified.? 

To George Smith, therefore, must be accorded the full credit, not only for his original 
discovery of the Flood-text, which aroused so great an enthusiasm throughout Europe, but for 
the groundwork on the twelve tablets of the Epic, with all the unseen care and patient labour 
which he expended on this series, for which he ultimately gave his life while on service for the 
British Museum in Mesopotamia. 

The subsequent history of the publication of this series, and the discovery of fresh texts, 
can be quickly and briefly followed. Fox Talbot commented on the Deluge (7. SBA. 1, 49, 
1875), and translated Tablet VI (ib. v, 97, 1877): St. Chad Boscawen translated Tablet XII 
(ib. Iv, 271, 1875): Francois Lenormant also discussed Smith’s discoveries (Les premzéres 
Civilisations, 1874). Thoroughly good composite texts of Tablets VI and XI were published 
by Sir Henry Rawlinson and George Smith in 1v &, 1875, with a second edition in 1891,

great advance. 

Then followed the identification of an Old Babylonian (Semitic) text of a tablet of the 
Epic (Meissner, 1ZV-AG. 1902, 1: discussed, Pinches, PSBA. xxv, 113, 1903), and Dhorme 
(Choix de Textes, 1907) again translated the Epic. A new fragment of the Assyrian Version 
(Tablet III) was published by King (Supp¢t. Kouyunjik Catalogue, No. 56, and PSBA. xxxvl, 
64, 1914), and a fresh translation of all the texts was made by Ungnad (Das Gzlgamesch-Epos, 
1911, and in his Kudturfragen, 4/5, 1923). Two more Old Babylonian tablets then came to 
light, Tablets II and III approximately, the first of which was noted by Poebel (OZZ. 1914, 4)

edited along with a first publication of the other by Jastrow and Clay (YOS. tv, 3, 1920).

We have naturally no knowledge of the authorship of the Epic. One Sin-liki-unni(n)ni ° 
is given as one of the editors on tablets of the seventh century (K. 9717 and S. 669), and the 
responsibility for the writing of the Ammizaduga Flood Tablet is on the shoulders of one 
Azag-Ai.

In Zablet VIII note that K. 8587 and K. 8565 give the beginnings and ends of Il. 1-10.

Of Tadlet X again Smith had identified most (except Rm. 621, not then recovered). I 
am inclined to think Sp. 299 should be here. 

Tablet XI, the Flood, is well known, thanks originally to Smith, followed by numerous 
commentators. The joins, S. 2131, S. 2196, Rm. ii, 383 and 390 together (K. 8518 to K. 8157, 
see Jensen, p. 228, n. 7), and the probable duplicate K. 16024, have added a little since the last 
publication. 

Much of Zadlet XII was known to George Smith. New joins are 81-2-4, 327 to K. 3475, 
while Rm. 933 and Rm. 964 are new additions. 

As regards the age of the Epic, it is certain that much now extant in Semitic dates at least 
to the beginning of the Second Millennium, and that there was a Sumerian edition, at least of 
part, much earlier. I do not propose to discuss this at length, but, as has long been suggested, 
it is a composite story. The episodes may be divided thus, the more striking points being 
stressed therein :! 

(i) Zhe Meeting of Gilgamish and Enkidu, when Erech represents civilization to the editor, 
who stresses the superiority of the city over the desert. It is noteworthy that here, as 
throughout the Epic, no mention is made of the date-palm, of which the groves (presuming that 
the palm was cultivated at the time of the composition of the Epic) must have been a 
common sight. 

(ii) Zhe Adventure with Lumbaba, with its introduction of the cedar, ev7nu, Cedrus Libant, 
which exists in large forests on the Taurus and Anti-Taurus. Gudea obtained them in the 
Amanus, describing their height as 60 and 50 cubits, as ayvainst that of the 25 cubits of the 


1 George Smith divided it under six headings: conclusion. See Jastrow, Religion, 494, 513. Jastrow 
(i) Introduction ; (ii) Meeting of Heabani and Izdubar ; and Clay, YOS. 1v. 3, would make Enkidu the older

ledge of the world possessed by the author of this portion. 

(iii) Zhe Adventure with Ishtar, which appears like an interpolation. The character of 
Ishtar is entirely different from that given in Tablet XI. One noteworthy point is the intro- 
duction of the ad/al/u-bird, the Roller, found in the palm-groves of S. Babylonia, but not (as 
far as I am aware) far north of Basrah, which points to a southern origin for the incident. 

(iv) Zhe Death of Enkidu, and the Search for Eternal Life. This latter idea is surely 
foreign to the earlier part of the story where such philosophical motzfs have no place, and yet 
there is no doubt that this section existed in the ancient Semitic texts. It leads up, through 
the hedonistic and /aisser-aller philosophy of the ale-wife, and the opposition of Ur-Shanabi, 
to the Flood story, which is somewhat artificially made a peg whereon to hang the immortality 
of Uta-napishtim, not the only suspicious detail in the story. Why should an ale-wife, one 
who makes and provides strong drink, live in remote parts of the sea? What is the thicket: 
so convenient to Ur-Shanabi on the shores of the sea (tidal waters of the marshes) where 120 
poles can be cut? Why should the plant which can bring back youth be allowed so short an 
adventure? The XIth tablet would seem to provide the original ending for the story with 
the safe return of Gilgamish to the city; yet here again, what reference has the expression of 
his satisfaction on the walls of Erech to the tragic result of his heroic search ? 

(v) Tablet XII, the Raising of Enkidu from the Dead, is entirely out of keeping with the 
rest. The tablet itself has a different appearance from the others, and is incidentally shorter. 
It is a strange finale, ending abruptly with a rather banal conclusion, which makes the whole 
tablet appear as an appendix. 

Apart from the story of the Flood, comparisons between the legends of Gilgamish and the 
classical myths, with Alexander’s journey to Mount Masius, or Musas, the Mas of Tablet IX, 
and even, as Sir John Miles suggests to me, the story of Valentine and Orson, which is said 
to be a French romance at least as early as the fifteenth century a.p. There is, however, no 
space here to go deeply into the different legends similar to our story, real or fancied ;* but 
there seems to be very tangible evidence of the story, or at least an incident pictured in it, 
having reached Egypt at very early times from the portrayal of the figure fighting two lions 
on the handle of the prehistoric knife from Gebel-el-’ Arak in Egypt,° that is, if the scene on 
the cylinder seals of Babylonia depicting a hero wrestling with lions is really intended for 
Gilgamish.

Gilgamish, as king of Erech, is found in the early Babylonian kings lists. In the Weld- 
Blundell list his immediate parentage is given thus: 

‘Enmekar, son of MeskemgaSer, king of Erech, who built Erech, became king; he reigned 420 years. 
The deified Lugalbanda, a shepherd, reigned 1,200 years. 

The deified Dumuzi (Tammuz), a fisherman, whose city was Habur, reigned 100 years. 

The deified Gilgami8, whose father was a /i/-/d, the lord of Kullab,° reigned 126 years. 

Ur-Nungal, the son of Gilgami§, reigned 30 years.’ 

Now the mother of Gilgamish is well known as the goddess Ninsun,* the consort of 
Lugalbanda, but the name of his father is still unsettled. Jensen, by a curious mistake which 
is discussed in the notes to Tablet I, Col. II, held that the break on Sp. 355, l. 7, represented 
the place where the name of his father had been (AZ. v1, xvit), but as this tablet is merely a 
duplicate of the beginning of what is left of Col. II, it has nothing to do with the name of the 
hero’s father ; and his error (curiously enough with the tablet itself) has been allowed to sink 
into oblivion. Next Fortsch in OZZ. 1915, 367 saw the beginning of the nameasA...., 
taking it from Poebel’s kings list, where the father of Gilgamish is described as a-... the 
lord of Kullab.?_ But this looks so much like the new Weld-Blundell text,’ which gives /2/-dé, 
that whatever a-.. may represent, we are entitled to depend on the 4/-/é as the father of 
Gilgamish. 

Now Langdon ® takes this //-/4 to mean ‘fool, imbecile, cripple’, but my own view is that 
it is the Sumerian “/-/é = “ld, the demon equivalent to a male vampire. There are four 
demons of this class—the zd/u Ulz, the ardat “/@, the “li, and the “/#tu, The ardat Lilt is 
well known as the female vampire or swccuéa who visits men by night and bears him ghostly 
children: the za/« “/# must be her male counterpart who can visit women and beget offspring 
by them, just as demigods are created. I suggest that the L/Z.LA, “li (probably not far 
different, if at all, from the za/@ “i, just as KJ.EL.LIL.LA.EN.NA = Uilitu, and not 
ardat lilt, CT, xvi, 4, 155) is the father of Gilgamish by the goddess Ninsun in spite of her 
marriage to Lugalbanda." For it is most remarkable that Gilgamish is described as ‘two- 
thirds god, one-third human’, and to the mathematically minded this is an impossibility, no 
matter how far back one of his progenitors was divine, provided that he or she mated with a 
full mortal. 

The problem thus raised casts doubt on the full-human paternity of his father, and there- 
fore it is conceivable that the 4“ who was his father was a male vampire and neither fully 
divine nor mortal, by whatever daemonogenetic Mendelism the result may have been brought 
about. Langdon is therefore right in noting in Aelian’s story about Gilgamos how his mother

‘secretly bore a son by an obscure man’, but the ‘obscure man’? is not an imbecile (Z//z), but 
a demon (L/Z.LA). 

It is not easy to find parallels in Semitic for a male vampire mating with a goddess, but 
it is legitimate to quote Pirke R. £7., xxi, xxi, that Cain’s real father was not Adam, but one 
of the demons, and here it is conceivable that the actual demon was the serpent. But what 
seems much nearer is the story of the paternity of Alexander, whose legends bear comparison, 
as is well known, with those of Gilgamish. Nectanebus, who had a reputation as a magician, 
is the reputed father of Alexander by Olympias, whom he is said to have visited in the form 
of a snake, a frequent guise for demons in Oriental mythology.? 

Naturally Gilgamish was in later times regarded as a god. As George Smith saw, he is 
mentioned ona tablet containing prayers to him as a god,’ and ‘on a series of tablets relating 
to witchcraft’,* and also on omen-texts.25 An image of him is mentioned in a late Assyrian 
letter,® and a drug is compounded of zzmzd(t) 46 ““Grlgamis (KAR. 186, r. 10). 

The name of Enkidu, the comrade of Gilgamish who was created by Aruru, is written 
it n-ki-du(g) (OB. Semitic), En-ki-du(g) (Sumerian),” ™*En-ki-tum (Boghaz-Keui Semitic), 
ME nkituS (Hittite), and *#En-ki-du(kak) (4V.). The first means either ‘ Enki (Ea) is good’, 
or (on the analogy of AV.) perhaps ‘ Enki has procreated’ (Br. No. 8232). In CZ. xviul, 30, 
I-II, 10, the equation 4.DU.V/I = En-gi-du shows that the reading is certain.s The 
name was formerly read Eabani or Heabani. 

In bringing this brief Introduction to a close, I should mention that a few fragments of 
tablets which I have met with in the course of my examination have been included among the 
texts, but they do not necessarily belong to the Gilgamish Epic. These are indicated by round 
brackets enclosing their numbers. One (Rm. 504, Pl. 29) is a new duplicate of the Epic of 
Creation, 1, 66, ff., which settles the reading w-ka/ (l. 72, see Langdon, OCT. v1, 90, 72): and 
a new duplicate of the Agum text (VR. 33, Rm. 505, Pl. 36). 

I wish to express my thanks for my great indebtedness to the Trustees of the British 
Museum for their permission to edit these texts, and to Sir Frederic Kenyon, G.B.E., K.C.B., 
D.Litt., the Director, for many courtesies and much sympathy extended to me during the work. 
Much also do I owe my good friends, Dr. H. R. Hall, the Keeper of the Department of 
Egyptian and Assyrian Antiquities, and his assistants, Mr. Sidney Smith and Mr. C. J. Gadd, 
who have in every way helped me. To the generosity of Professor Langdon I owe the in- 
clusion of a new tablet which restores much of Tablet I, Col. I, for which I am very grateful.

TABLET I (Assyrian Version) 


Cot. I,1. The name of this Epic is indicated in the Colophons according to custom by 
an abbreviation of the first line Sa xakba imuru, and it is also given the title therein KU. KAR 
™Gilgamis. It is uncertain whether anything more than this catch-line is to be supplied in the 
big gap at the beginning, but surely we should expect a mention of the hero’s name at or before 
the end of 1. 4. Makéa has two possible meanings, the one usually adopted being ‘all’; as 
George Smith (CG. 173) pointed out, the other, ‘channel’ or ‘river-bed’, is a possibility, and 
this cannot be entirely ruled out. As Mr. C. J. Gadd suggested to me, the excavators of Ur 
have been finding the mud bed of primitive waters (cf. 1. 6). 

[Luseldaz, 111, 1 (2d ‘to know’); or should it be [wse]ddz ? 

6. That the Flood was accepted in Assyria as a definite event is clear also from 4 JZ. 105, 
21, ‘the arcana of the seers, by whose... it is made known from the speech of the ancient 
sages before the Flood which was in Shuruppak’. In the Kings’ List (Langdon, OE CT. n, 9) 
we find ‘the Flood came: after the Flood had come, the rulership descended from heaven.’ 
See also Langdon’s note, zézd., and for another reference, Langdon, Le poéme, 147, nn. I, 2, 
quoting S.4sd. 11, 256, 18 and v. &. 44, I, 20. 

g. Here begins the new part of the tablet B.M. No. 34916, which Professor Langdon so 
generously indicated to me. The lines are repeated for convenience (for variants see p. 1): 


g. u-Se-piS dari 8a Uruk* su-pu-ri

Here the fragment breaks off, but the text is obviously much the same as that at the end 
of the XIth tablet, which is certainly another piece of evidence that the XIIth tablet was an 
addition to the accepted story. The translation of these lines runs: 


9. He built the rampart of Erech, the high-wall’d, 
10. Of sacred E-Anna, the holy treasury (v. storehouse), 
11. Beheld its rampart which had been built like brass, 
12. Discover’d its base which none had rivall’d, 
13. Grasp’d the threshold which was of ancient times, 
14. Drew nigh to E-Anna, the dwelling of Ishtar, 
15. Of the kings who went before not one had rivall’d (it), 
16. He went up and took counsel‘ on the rampart of Erech, 
17. Look’d at its base, and took heed of the bricks, 
18. The foundation of its bricks was not kiln-burnt, 
19. Its ground-work was not bitumen, seven courses.

the circumstantial clause in |. 7, zékamma anth. For details of the internal economy of 
E-Anna, see Tremayne, Kecords from Erech.

No. 34248 (Sp. 355) was first published (in transliteration) by Jensen (AZ. v1, 1, xvm), 
‘ Vielleicht zum Gilgami8-Epos gehért—und dann wohl zu Tafel I Col. I—, und den Namen des

1,12) being merely ava. The line containing the supposed name is merely ‘ Gilgamish [left no] 
son to [his] fa{ther]’. 

10. Luna pukku Sutebt could mean ‘they were forced out of the net’, but the sense 
demands ‘into the net’. The picture of Eannatum, king of Lagash ¢. 3050 B.c., forcing his 
enemies into his net provides the explanation. For pukku, kubbutum, and mikki as values for 
the sign LAGAB (‘an enclosure’) see CZ. xxxv, 1, 16: Langdon, RA. 1915, 77, kima pukku 
a mikké belit tahazi Sutamhisu tamkaru. On mikkii = musahhiptum ‘hunter's net’, Langdon 
(zbed.) quotes 11. R 22, 6.10. Mrkki@ occurs herein Tab. XII, 2, 5, 57, 64, also with the idea 
of striking to earth. The sense ‘zz/o the net’ is paralleled by sadvru dimnu tna elippi-kunu lusatba, 
Winckler, /orsch. 11, 11, p. 12.

13. Or ckaddir se-e(?)-[rz-3u]. With kadéru, cf. kadru ‘arrogant’ (SAsé. s.v.), and 1. 20 
farther on: cf. Syr. addir ‘great’ (KAR. 194, obv. I, 19, 4a-ad-ra-a-a-t-tum) : ‘[his] flesh 
groweth (?)’, or, less probably, reading se-7[z¢-su] ‘his rage’. 

14. Uruk" supuri ‘ Erech of the (cattle) enclosure’ here with reference to its great wall, 
paralleled by vzézt2 ‘of the city-square, the mazdén’ in the OB. versions, and distinct from 
Uruk* métum. Jensen, in his review, OLZ. 1929, 645, quotes also Akkad réditim (Hammu- 
rabi’s Laws, obv. iv, 51). The -#a in r2’u-ma must have some such force as I have given it. 

18-19. J. (119), Dh. (72. 187), U. (GE. 7) suggest that the ‘gods of heaven’ are the 
‘lords of Erech’. But 4é2 Uruk* is singular (cf. G. Smith, CG. 199, 4) and 1. 20 shows that 
some one is addressed. The ‘gods of heaven’ can hardly be lords of Erech; Anu might per- 
haps be (so E. AOT.), as being god of E-Anna, but |. 20 ‘thou hast caused a son to be’ 
looks as though it must at least be an ancestor, if not his father. 7Z7/¢adSz is an unusual word, 
and so it might perhaps be Lugalbanda (see Introduction p. 9) who could be considered ‘lord of 
Erech’ (as a guardian, cf. U. GZ. 78). It will be observed that while zs¢exzmme in 1. 29 is 
singular, zss@ in 1. 30 is apparently plural: it is, however, possible that Anu is to be supplied 
in 1. 29. 

go. Aruru, with Marduk (82—5-22, 1048, Pinches, RAS. 1891, 400) created the seed of 
mankind. She is the protectress of babes (Deimel, Panthéon, 59), sister of Enlil, and aunt of the 
god Lilla, the son of Enlil (Langdon, Le poéme, 23). GAL-tu, after zss#, can be read radétu, 
and may refer to Aruru; but placed thus after the verb should be a predicate (cf. 1. 35), which 
lacks force here if it is an adjective, and even if itis a noun it is difficult. The Assyrian would 
have said ‘the great Aruru’ (even then a banal adjective) or ‘the lady Aruru’ ina less cumbrous 
way. It is not impossible that ~adz¢w, the abstract, should be read here, like the OB. (Penns. 
Ill, 33) 2¢¢2[lu] naki[ dé] va-bu-tum ‘the shepherds slept soundly’, and translated ‘they called 
loudly’, although Jensen (OZZ. 1929, 646) says that this is impossible. 

33. The zeéru of Anu must surely be the conception in Anu’s mind, and not necessarily 
the model on which Aruru makes Enkidu. Cf. e¢kru $a néri ‘concept of the slaying’, Tablet 
VILL Vida. Ps: 

34. /kéarzs, the usual word for taking clay (cf. J7A. s.v.), doubtless a technical potter’s 
word. J¢éadz, lit. ‘she threw’ may again be a potter’s word, ‘ throwing’ on the wheel (in order 
to make the clay stick firmly) being the first action in pot-turning. Other translations have 
been given: J. (121) ‘threw (him) on the desert’: U. (GZ. 8: RBA. 69) ‘spat (?) thereon’: 
E. (AOT. 151) ‘marked(?) thereon’. Or possibly z¢¢ad7/ has an intransitive meaning ‘she 
broke off some clay (as) it lay on the desert”, as an indefinite relative clause (but in this case 
without -z) (see for this the note to Col. V, 26). 

35. ttc 2irt? seems to mean an offspring ‘born (and) begotten’ (a/édu and zeré).

26. Suttu attulu ‘a dream (which) | saw’. Note the relative clause with -, without Sa, 
when the subject of the main clause is indefinite, just as it is in Arabic: cf. Wright, dvad.

Rm. 289, 1 (= O#8. u, Penns. v) 35 ‘{[Enkidu] in the street of Erech, [the high-wall’d] 
(36) .. . deed of power and.. (37) he block’d the way . . . (38) The land of Erech stood [over

A comparison of this fragment with the Penns. tablet suggests that this is a very much 
curtailed and bald edition of the vigorous OB. story. L. 40, see Tablet I, v, 33; 1. 41, 
ug(k)t(d)ammaru, cf. the Arab. ammara ‘assemble’; |. 42, read #-na-[ak(?)], II, 1 of enéhu, but it 
does not occur elsewhere. L. 44, the use of the name of the goddess [Shara has been entirely 
misunderstood. JC. assume (p. 84) that I8hara is the spouse of Gilgamish : U. (GZ. 16) and E. 
(A OT. 156) take it as a goddess, the latter saying ‘GilgameS soll wohl der Gatte der Gottin 
werden’. But, as I mentioned in Man, 1928, 14, the goddess particularly presides over 
sexual intercourse (as a form of Ishtar), since she is appealed to by name in the love-charms 
(ibid., and Ebeling, Lzedeszauber, 43, 12). The use of the goddess’s name here is intended, 
by a very common transference of ideas, to indicate sexual intercourse, just as ™Gibil is ‘fire’, 
"Nisaba ‘corn’, and is entirely impersonal. L. 45, sa-:-[2-su], from O#. u, Penns. v, 27. 
L. 48, 6é¢ emuti may be perhaps either ‘Community house’, the common meeting-place of the 
men of the tribe (emutu = pubru, kissatu, MA. 63), or else, having regard to the word emu 
‘father in law’, it may have some closer matrimonial connexion. (Cf. my Devz/s, 1, 32, 29, 
ttla ina bit emutt-sa usesiét ‘they drive out the hero from his d¢¢ emutz’.) It is at this d¢¢ emutz, 
as also in the Penns. text, that Enkidu meets Gilgamish, preventing his entry, presumably to 
defend the hetaera from him (see p. 76). L. 49, eétigra IV, 2 from garu. The end of the 
line suggests Penns. vi. 11, but is doubtful. L. 50, 2-¢#-ws at end looks as if it should be 
2-na-us or 2t-tu-us, from nasi ‘ quake’ ; otherwise possibly z¢ws ‘its wall’. 


Cox. III, 48. - Rz-sat-"NIN .(L/L}, or as Poebel (OLZ. 1914, 4: U., ZA. 1917, 52:

Cot. III, 5. ['“Au|wawa, the ““ffumbaba of AV., presumed to be an Elamite first by 
George Smith (CG. 185) on account of the first part of the name Humba, who is an Elamite 
god (see also, for the latest evidence, Sidney Smith, Aznad/s, x1, 109). It is the same word as 
4u-wa-wa in the omen text, CZ. xxvu, 21, 8, Zumbadbitu, ib, 418, and the variant ““WUMZ. 
fIUM is rightly accepted by Clay (Bad. Doc. tn Library of 7. Pierpont Morgan, wv, no. 13), 
and has been compared to Kopféos, the guardian of Stratonike (Lucian, De Dea Syria, 19), 
Humbaba being the guardian of the Forest of Cedars. It actually occurs as a personal name 
(HZuwawa, Chiera, Lis¢, 122). From the curious representation of his face in clay he is 
obviously an ogre (Sidney Smith, ibid., and Thureau-Dangin, RA. xxu, 1925, 23), the purpose 
being to provide the augur with a comparison with the entrails which he is observing, the 
first omen on the reverse of the representation being ‘If the entrails [resemble] the face of 
Huwawa, it is the omen of Sargon who ruled the land’. Smith quotes also a passage from 
Ebeling (7VAG. 1918, 11. 6, 21) where ‘a suppliant pleads to Tammuz that various evil 
spirits who have attacked him may be entrusted to the keeping of “the mighty Humbaba, the 
devil who hath no compassion”.’ Haupt suggests (see Jastrow-Clay, YOS. 1v, 3, 21) that 
Enkidu, in speaking of Humbaba, is describing a volcano. In this connexion cf. the burning 
petroleum fires of Kirkuk Baba.

TABLET Vi 


1. Mak ‘stains’, E. ‘Schmutz’: G. Smith ‘disease’ (in Tablet XI, CG. 274, 275) 
HWB.‘ Beule’. Cf. C7. xxvu, 23, 15 ‘If a sheep bears a lion and ma--2 na-st’: K. 2063, 
Summa kakkad amelu ma-lu-u i-lap-pi- .. . (Bezold, Catalogue, s.n.). Daiches (ZA. xvtt, 93) 
ingeniously compared it to the Heb. m’/éfém ‘rags’: see Streck, Babyloniaca, u, 183. From 
Tablet XI, 240, they can be washed off in water, so that it would look as if ‘stain ’ is correct. 

2. Unasis: cf. Tablet VII, IV, 4, and the passages in 7A. s.v., particularly massusu $a 
ztbbati ‘ braiding (?) of the tail’, i.e., with reference to the careful combing and tying of the 
Assyrian horses’ tails (on the sculptures). 

4. Asét: J. and U. give no equivalent: Albright, RA. 1919, 177, ‘tunic’: Bezold 
(Glossary) ‘ Uberwurf’: see Zimmern, ZA. xxxvi, 1925, 319, ‘tassel’, but doubtful. 

7. Liu far, indefinite state of predicate without case-ending. 

ur. Elmesu. Haupt’s suggestion (FA OS. xxi, 118) that this was the same as the Heb. 
hasmal (Ezek. 1, 4, 27) was correct. It cannot be ‘diamond’ (apart from the great doubt 
whether the ancient Assyrians knew the diamond). asma/, as he says, has been taken to 
mean ‘electrum’, and that is what must be meant here. Haupt’s brilliant suggestion does not 
appear to have been generally adopted. 

Electrum is a mixture of silver and gold, known to the ancients for its particularly brilliant 
quality (Pliny, WH. xxxiu, 23, ‘one peculiar advantage of electrum is its superior brilliancy 
to silver in lamplight’). The brilliancy of e/mesu is referred to in 1V RX. 61 [68], m1, 33, uur 
Sa tlmesi ina pan "A Sur-ah-iddin sar "A Sur usanamara ‘1 will make shine the light of 
zlmesu in the face of Esarhaddon, king of Assyria’. It occurs in the tablet giving long lists of 
metals, &c. (Scheil, RA. 1921, 52, 19) when, after amounts of gold, silver, copper, lead, bronze, 
‘living copper’, one shekel of e/meSu is mentioned. There is no reason, as Haupt saw in 
F AOS., to consider it as a stone. 

In the present instance Ishtar refers, doubtless, to the custom of overlaying wood with 
electrum, as was the custom in Egypt (Lucas, Ancient Eg. Materials, 86). If the ed at the 
beginning of e¢/mesu is an indication that it is an Arabic word and that electrum originally came 
from Arabia, the Arabic word mdésa ‘mingled with’ is a plausible derivation: a/més ‘the 
mixture’. But the & of Zasmad is against this.

Cox. I, 1. Siduri, called Sadztu, the woman who dwells za sapan tdémti (not cna kussi, as 
Jensen inferred from Bezold’s copy in the Catalogue of the Cuneiform Tablets, p. 436), i.e. ‘the 
depths of the sea’, the remotest part, is the typical hedonist or siren of the stories who tries 
to turn Gilgamish aside from his long journey with the lure of pleasure. Her secondary name, 
Sabitu, was in earlier days taken to mean Sabaean (Jensen, AKA. vi, 470; Hommel, Altisr. 
Uberlicferung, 35); but as far back as Muss-Arnolt (1905) the Aram. sdboyaéthé ‘barmaids’ 
was suggested as a comparison, and this has since been accepted (see Zimmern, ZA. 1918, 
169): the male “Sa-dz-2 is found in Ebeling, VAG. 1917, 1, 19, sadé@ being an intoxi- 
cating drink. To szdurz a parallel has been found in s:durz = ‘maiden’ (Jensen, AZ. v1, 578), 
but it is not convincing.

28. Ur-Sanabi, or as the Semitic Old Babylonian text calls him, Sur-sunabu, ‘the servant 
of Ea’ is the boatman of Uta-napishtim. Not dissimilarly from Siduri, he is instrumental in 
suggesting the difficulties of the journey to Gilgamish who has destroyed an essential part of 


( 85 ) 


the boat which is to take them over the waters of Death, a kind of Scylla and Charybdis, 
where apparently the water kills if it touches the hands. 

29. SU.UT.TAK.MES,a great problem. It is usually taken as su-wt abné?! (‘ welche 
mit Steinen’ (J.): ‘die Stein-...’ (U.), ‘a chest full of stones’ (Haupt, 7AOS. xxxu, 8, 
followed by E.): Luckenbill suggests a kind of kedge or anchor of stones (4 ¥. SZ. xxxvitl, 97). 
But the boat must be a light skiff or de//am, such as the Arabs of the southern marshes use to 
this day, either poled or paddled. Stones (even as an anchor) would have little place therein. 
It can hardly be a Semitic word su-z¢-tak* from edéku. It occurs only in this Epic (and only 
in plural) : a possible parallel is sa-u¢ rész ‘ general’. 

Gilgamish, by his destruction of them, hinders his journey, and thereafter, in order to 
proceed, he must cut poles to punt the boat. Then as they come in sight of Uta-napishtim, 
the latter sees that the [SU. UT. TAK. ME S| are destroyed. 

Then, of course, the most obvious explanation would be ‘sails’, visible from afar (as in 
the story of Aegeus and the forgotten sail of Theseus); probably of reed or fibre from the 
copse, not at all times of the year available, but it is hard to find a philological equivalence. 
Nor can wvnu be connected with any known plant-name, for wrnxd@ (long z) ‘ mint’ (or similar) 
is impossible here.

Cox. III, 41. Partsu: cf. the new Esarhaddon prism from Nineveh, Col. IV, 84, Au-um 
imrsqsé?!_e sa-an-du par-ri-sa-ni ‘are yoked rowers instead of horses’ (to their ships). The 
parisu must here be a punting-pole and not a paddle (although the de//am or mashhiif, the 
skiff of the Babylonian marshes, use both) for the following reasons :—(1) Gilgamish apparently 
makes 120 of them, so that he may throw each away after it is wet, that the ‘ Waters of Death’ 
may not touch him (if this is the way the incident is to be explained): this would not be 
necessary for a paddle in which the handle can always be kept dry; (2) the fashioning of the 
poles ‘ Bitumen (them) and put a socket’ (kupur-ma Sukun tulad), The punting-pole of the 
Mesopotamian skiff to-day has a knob of bitumen at the upper end (called £¢va) and a metal 
socket or ferule (called legma = lakma) at the lower end. With ¢w/é cf. Syriac ?/azthé ‘handle’ 
and Heb. ?/u ‘handle’, and Aram. ¢adyé ‘plug’.

regnen lassen werdex’: U. ‘Die Regenten(?) der Finsternis(?) werden eines Abends (?)

Kukku, as J. (485) points out, is the equivalent for the character GUG (Lr. No. 6916), 
which has also the values dalifu, mardsu, |rahdsu| (?, SAT. 4922), and [dalélu)], RA. 1913, 76, 
so that the accepted meaning ‘darkness’ is satisfactory, with which the,Syriac £awkithé ‘ storm, 
darkness’ is comparable. But, as J. remarks, who can the senders of darkness be? We have 
in ll. 87, 90 to show that a particularly obvious occasion (adannw) is foretold, and actually 
occurs, so that Uta-napishtim knows that the Flood is coming. At the same time, in the 
beginning, when he is warned to leave Shuruppak and build a boat, Ea provides him with an 
explanation to satisfy the townsfolk, so that they may be deceived to the last moment : 


‘LO MorlJtal (?), thus shalt thou say to them, 
“Me [alo]ne doth Enlil hate, and 
I may not dwell in your [city], 
Nor to Enlil’s dry land may I set my face: 
I will go down to the Deep, dwelling with Ea, my lord, 
“Tis on you he shall rain plenty, 
of birds, a lair for fishes 
ae increase (of) the harvest 
(It shall be) [on a morn] of storm, 
(When) the heavens shall pour down on you heavy rains (?) [in the twilight]”’. 


If we read zua Se-zy and not m-zr, it may mean on that stormy morning of winter, in the 
twilight (4/é¢¢) of which the god sends the rain. On the other hand, Frank (ZA. 1925, 218), 
has an ingenious and tempting explanation that the lines contain a play on words, £u&ki mean- 
ing both ‘bran’ and ‘darkness’, and -£26ét2 ‘wheat’ and ‘oppression’, so that while the 
townspeople think he is telling them that ‘the sender of bran will let rain down on them the 


eee) 
wheat-rain ’, he is really saying ‘the sender of darkness will let the showers rain on them 
oppression ’. 

I do not think that Langdon’s explanation that kukku = chickpeas, and s7zbatz = ‘wheat’ 
= hailstorm, is satisfactory.

215. Jensen (KZ. vi, 511) held that this difficult passage might indicate seven differ- 
ent loaves of bread. Haupt, I think more correctly, suggested (A ¥SZ. xxvi, 16) that it 
shows the making of bread. Gressmann (GZ. 141, note 4) follows Haupt: ‘Sicher scheint 
mir dass v. 224 ff., die verschiedenen Stadien des Brotbackens geschildert werden. Da termini 
technici vorliegen, sind sie fiir uns schwer verstandlich’. Jensen, however, is not ready to 
accept this (OZZ. 1929, 647) ‘Es ist nun durchaus méglich, dass an sechs Brote in verschie- 
denen Stadien ihres Entstehens und Vergehens zu denken ist.’ 

In spite of Gressmann’s concluding remark, it is possible, I think, to follow most of 
the process. In one point, however, I must admit I was wrong in translating kurummatu 
‘flour’ (as Jensen points out): the meaning here is the usual one, ‘rations’, ‘food’, and here 
particularly the vaticwm for the parting guest. I doubt if it can mean ‘bread’. 

There are several methods in vogue in the Near East of making bread; the commonest 
shape is that of the flat disk of thin bread varying in size, but usually about a foot in diameter. 
This is probably one of the earliest forms of bread, and it is shown on the Hittite Sculptures, 
where two kings (?) are sharing a feast with this bread piled up in front of them (for the picture, 
see Garstang, Land of the Hittites, pl. xxv); the rounds of bread are to-day piled up in a limp 
flat circular tray-basket (called sufrah). In S. Mesopotamia the oven (¢annur), either in 
bakers’ shops or on the decks of the larger boats, is roughly dome-shaped, either open at the 
top, or closed, having a circular orifice of 18” diameter at one side. The fuel is put in 
from the top, the fire flares up within, and the walls of the oven are thus heated. The baker 
then kneads the flour (¢aZ) in a green glazed bowl (madjen), or a copper bowl (called locally 
injanah), and after the kneading, the leaven (Zamrah, simple dough from yesterday's kneading) 
is added. If no leaven has been retained from yesterday, the dough is left unleavened. The 
baker then makes small cakes (sazak) of this dough, which he pats out to a thin disk about 
10” diameter, which he slaps from hand to hand, and this he plasters on the inner hot wall of 
the oven, where it is baked in about a minute. Ovens exactly like these were discovered by 
Dr. H. R. Hall in his excavations at Ur in 1919. 

The Arab of the Sinaitic desert makes his pancake of bread rather thicker, and cooks it 
in the ashes (the aka/ tumrz of p. 79). The more civilized Arab of the poorer class at Mosul 


( 88 ) 


bakes the pancake on a convex iron plate over the fire. Still more primitive is the native of 
the E. Sudan, who wraps his dough round stones and toasts it in front of the fire (see my 
Pilgrim’s Scrip, 162). For the process in general, see the excellent article ‘ Bread’ in 
Wm. Smith’s Dictionary of the Bible). In our text first the ‘rations’ (kurummatu) are 
§ab(p)us(s, Jat, a difficult word. Haupt (/¢. 25) compares Talmud s%éées ‘to confuse’, and 
translates ‘was made up’. Sabésu is used of dust, in Maklu, I, 133, and VI, §5, being collected 
for magic, with equivalent za-... in the Maku Commentary, KAR. 94, 11. Sad(p)ulu also 
is a possible comparison (preceded by vatéu, Br. 5205). But none of these find a satisfactory 
comparison in the other Semitic languages; the expected meaning might be ‘ground’ or 
‘rubbed into flour’. Next it is mussukat; cf SE.BA.LA.GIM = sim massikti (u. R. 33, 
16, g+ Rm. 619), perhaps = Syr. m’sak ‘become thin’ (i.e. ‘ground (?)’ or ‘sifted (?)’). But 
whatever be the processes described by Sadusat and mussukat, they precede the moistening 
(ratbat) when it becomes dough: it is then kneaded into a cake (<ptest hamansu, Heb. pasa’ 
‘crush’, Arab. fasa’a ‘rub’; hardly the ¢e-f2-2s of CZ. xxi, 43, 6, ‘thou shalt pound’, nor, I 
think, as Haupt, the Arab. dada’a ‘divide’). Then she adds S7ba ([sz]fa, 1. 227), which must 
be ‘leaven’ (not, I think, ‘grease’, as Haupt), connected with s#u ‘old’, the old piece from 
yesterday, the Syr. s2éh, in pael sayyed ‘ contaminavit’ (comparable to the effect of leaven, cf. 
W. Smith, Dzc/. of Bible, ii, 85). Lastly it is baked (Haupt correctly). Note Exod. xii, 34, 
that the people took their dough before it was leavened; and, if we are to take the ‘seven 
days’ of Uta-napishtim seriously, it must be remembered that the leavened mass was allowed 
in other parts of the Near East to stand for some time, even for a whole night (Matt. xiii, 33 ; 
Luke xiii, 21; Hos. vii, 6, quoted Smith, Dzct. of Bridle, I, 227). 

220. An-ni-mis (Sit, rid) or an (for ana) ni-Sit. Adverbial? See Haupt, 7A. xxxu. 64. 

221. Taddikannt, 1, 2 from adtké (with J.) ‘rouse’. But edakka in line 224 must be from 
the same root. 

231. /kkimu ‘a robber’, not easily explicable. 

237. Lktasu-u, parallel to masku-u in line 238, asking a question. 

259. Lllika(ka) tnaha tSuta ‘cometh (goeth) all weary with rowing’, three verbs. sua, 
cf. Heb. Sayzt ‘ rowing’ and sétim ‘rowers’ (J.). Actually he had been poling (if Jarvzsu is a 
punt-pole). 

272. Gilgamish follows the custom of the pearl-divers of Bahrain, long celebrated for its 
pearl-fisheries. In 1916 it fell to my lot in Basrah to interrogate some pearl-merchants who 
wished to return to Lingah, and they told me that the diver descends with a clip on his nose, 
and with one foot in the loop of a rope to which is attached a heavy stone, weighing about 
four Stambul okes at one end, the other end being held by a friend above. The basket for 
collecting the pearls is slung about the diver’s neck, and he has also another rope attached to 
him, which is also held by his friend above, by which he signals to be hauled up. I was told 
that the men could go down to a depth of twenty fathoms, and make forty descents in the hour, 
which seems an exaggeration. One of the divers spoke with an habitual breathlessness, and 
had been burnt in front of the ears, probably to cure some ear trouble. Percival, in his 
Account of the Island of Ceylon (1803), gives an account of the divers there using large stones 
to accelerate their descent, the other details being similar to the above. 

289. Kulultum, perhaps kuliptum, with Albright, RA. 1919, 189, ‘it shed a skin’, that 
is, exhibiting a fresh youth after eating the plant. See also Jensen, OZLZ. 1929, 650, and I 
am now more inclined to agree with it. At the same time, £u/zptc-sw would have surely 
been preferable.

Tablet VI, 68, but hardly the Arab. 3.
